The plan fixes a VIP formula bug. The current code uses np.var(y) instead of per-component y_loadings_, which produces skewed wavelength rankings. The plan should: (a) replace with canonical Wold (2001) formula using y_loadings_, (b) include a regression test that distinguishes new formula from old, (c) be detailed enough that GLM 5.1 can implement unattended.

REVIEW FOCUS:
1. Mathematical correctness of the new VIP formula. Verify it matches Wold (2001) / Mehmood et al. (2012) canonical form: VIP_j = sqrt(p * sum_a(SS_a * w_aj^2 / norm(w_a)^2) / sum_a(SS_a)) where SS_a = q_a^2 * t_a'*t_a (sum of squared y_loadings × scores).
2. Test design — does the test actually distinguish the OLD np.var(y) formula from the NEW y_loadings formula on the synthetic problem? Or could both formulas produce similar rankings by accident?
3. Edge cases — what about a 1-component PLS model? What about zero y_loadings_? Plan should handle.
4. Backward compatibility — does anything in the codebase consume VIP rankings expecting the old (incorrect) ordering? Spot check.
5. Effort estimate (30 min from roadmap) realistic?

description: Use when completing tasks, implementing major features, or before merging to verify work meets requirements

# Requesting Code Review

Dispatch superpowers:code-reviewer subagent to catch issues before they cascade. The reviewer gets precisely crafted context for evaluation — never your session's history. This keeps the reviewer focused on the work product, not your thought process, and preserves your own context for continued work.

**Core principle:** Review early, review often.

## When to Request Review

**Mandatory:**
- After each task in subagent-driven development
- After completing major feature
- Before merge to main

**Optional but valuable:**
- When stuck (fresh perspective)
- Before refactoring (baseline check)
- After fixing complex bug

## How to Request

**1. Get git SHAs:**
```bash
BASE_SHA=$(git rev-parse HEAD~1)  # or origin/main
HEAD_SHA=$(git rev-parse HEAD)
```

**2. Dispatch code-reviewer subagent:**

Use Task tool with superpowers:code-reviewer type, fill template at `code-reviewer.md`

**Placeholders:**
- `{WHAT_WAS_IMPLEMENTED}` - What you just built
- `{PLAN_OR_REQUIREMENTS}` - What it should do
- `{BASE_SHA}` - Starting commit
- `{HEAD_SHA}` - Ending commit
- `{DESCRIPTION}` - Brief summary

**3. Act on feedback:**
- Fix Critical issues immediately
- Fix Important issues before proceeding
- Note Minor issues for later
- Push back if reviewer is wrong (with reasoning)

## Example

```
[Just completed Task 2: Add verification function]

You: Let me request code review before proceeding.

BASE_SHA=$(git log --oneline | grep "Task 1" | head -1 | awk '{print $1}')
HEAD_SHA=$(git rev-parse HEAD)

[Dispatch superpowers:code-reviewer subagent]
  WHAT_WAS_IMPLEMENTED: Verification and repair functions for conversation index
  PLAN_OR_REQUIREMENTS: Task 2 from docs/superpowers/plans/deployment-plan.md
  BASE_SHA: a7981ec
  HEAD_SHA: 3df7661
  DESCRIPTION: Added verifyIndex() and repairIndex() with 4 issue types

[Subagent returns]:
  Strengths: Clean architecture, real tests
  Issues:
    Important: Missing progress indicators
    Minor: Magic number (100) for reporting interval
  Assessment: Ready to proceed

You: [Fix progress indicators]
[Continue to Task 3]
```

## Integration with Workflows

**Subagent-Driven Development:**
- Review after EACH task
- Catch issues before they compound
- Fix before moving to next task

**Executing Plans:**
- Review after each batch (3 tasks)
- Get feedback, apply, continue

**Ad-Hoc Development:**
- Review before merge
- Review when stuck

## Red Flags

**Never:**
- Skip review because "it's simple"
- Ignore Critical issues
- Proceed with unfixed Important issues
- Argue with valid technical feedback

**If reviewer wrong:**
- Push back with technical reasoning
- Show code/tests that prove it works
- Request clarification

See template at: requesting-code-review/code-reviewer.md

<SUBAGENT-STOP>
If you were dispatched as a subagent to execute a specific task, skip this skill.
</SUBAGENT-STOP>

<EXTREMELY-IMPORTANT>
If you think there is even a 1% chance a skill might apply to what you are doing, you ABSOLUTELY MUST invoke the skill.

IF A SKILL APPLIES TO YOUR TASK, YOU DO NOT HAVE A CHOICE. YOU MUST USE IT.

This is not negotiable. This is not optional. You cannot rationalize your way out of this.
</EXTREMELY-IMPORTANT>

## Instruction Priority

Superpowers skills override default system prompt behavior, but **user instructions always take precedence**:

1. **User's explicit instructions** (CLAUDE.md, GEMINI.md, AGENTS.md, direct requests) — highest priority
2. **Superpowers skills** — override default system behavior where they conflict
3. **Default system prompt** — lowest priority

If CLAUDE.md, GEMINI.md, or AGENTS.md says "don't use TDD" and a skill says "always use TDD," follow the user's instructions. The user is in control.

## How to Access Skills

**In Claude Code:** Use the `Skill` tool. When you invoke a skill, its content is loaded and presented to you—follow it directly. Never use the Read tool on skill files.

**In Copilot CLI:** Use the `skill` tool. Skills are auto-discovered from installed plugins. The `skill` tool works the same as Claude Code's `Skill` tool.

**In Gemini CLI:** Skills activate via the `activate_skill` tool. Gemini loads skill metadata at session start and activates the full content on demand.

**In other environments:** Check your platform's documentation for how skills are loaded.

## Platform Adaptation

Skills use Claude Code tool names. Non-CC platforms: see `references/copilot-tools.md` (Copilot CLI), `references/codex-tools.md` (Codex) for tool equivalents. Gemini CLI users get the tool mapping loaded automatically via GEMINI.md.

# Using Skills

## The Rule

**Invoke relevant or requested skills BEFORE any response or action.** Even a 1% chance a skill might apply means that you should invoke the skill to check. If an invoked skill turns out to be wrong for the situation, you don't need to use it.

```dot
digraph skill_flow {
    "User message received" [shape=doublecircle];
    "About to EnterPlanMode?" [shape=doublecircle];
    "Already brainstormed?" [shape=diamond];
    "Invoke brainstorming skill" [shape=box];
    "Might any skill apply?" [shape=diamond];
    "Invoke Skill tool" [shape=box];
    "Announce: 'Using [skill] to [purpose]'" [shape=box];
    "Has checklist?" [shape=diamond];
    "Create TodoWrite todo per item" [shape=box];
    "Follow skill exactly" [shape=box];
    "Respond (including clarifications)" [shape=doublecircle];

    "About to EnterPlanMode?" -> "Already brainstormed?";
    "Already brainstormed?" -> "Invoke brainstorming skill" [label="no"];
    "Already brainstormed?" -> "Might any skill apply?" [label="yes"];
    "Invoke brainstorming skill" -> "Might any skill apply?";

    "User message received" -> "Might any skill apply?";
    "Might any skill apply?" -> "Invoke Skill tool" [label="yes, even 1%"];
    "Might any skill apply?" -> "Respond (including clarifications)" [label="definitely not"];
    "Invoke Skill tool" -> "Announce: 'Using [skill] to [purpose]'";
    "Announce: 'Using [skill] to [purpose]'" -> "Has checklist?";
    "Has checklist?" -> "Create TodoWrite todo per item" [label="yes"];
    "Has checklist?" -> "Follow skill exactly" [label="no"];
    "Create TodoWrite todo per item" -> "Follow skill exactly";
}
```

## Red Flags

These thoughts mean STOP—you're rationalizing:

| Thought | Reality |
|---------|---------|
| "This is just a simple question" | Questions are tasks. Check for skills. |
| "I need more context first" | Skill check comes BEFORE clarifying questions. |
| "Let me explore the codebase first" | Skills tell you HOW to explore. Check first. |
| "I can check git/files quickly" | Files lack conversation context. Check for skills. |
| "Let me gather information first" | Skills tell you HOW to gather information. |
| "This doesn't need a formal skill" | If a skill exists, use it. |
| "I remember this skill" | Skills evolve. Read current version. |
| "This doesn't count as a task" | Action = task. Check for skills. |
| "The skill is overkill" | Simple things become complex. Use it. |
| "I'll just do this one thing first" | Check BEFORE doing anything. |
| "This feels productive" | Undisciplined action wastes time. Skills prevent this. |
| "I know what that means" | Knowing the concept ≠ using the skill. Invoke it. |

## Skill Priority

When multiple skills could apply, use this order:

1. **Process skills first** (brainstorming, debugging) - these determine HOW to approach the task
2. **Implementation skills second** (frontend-design, mcp-builder) - these guide execution

"Let's build X" → brainstorming first, then implementation skills.
"Fix this bug" → debugging first, then domain-specific skills.

## Skill Types

**Rigid** (TDD, debugging): Follow exactly. Don't adapt away discipline.

**Flexible** (patterns): Adapt principles to context.

The skill itself tells you which.

## User Instructions

Instructions say WHAT, not HOW. "Add X" or "Fix Y" doesn't mean skip workflows.

.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:56:> Scope: Determine whether each variable selection method x search path refits varsel inside CV folds (CLEAN) or computes once on full calibration data with a frozen list passed into CV (LEAKY).
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:70:**Every y-using variable selection method in every search path it actually runs in is LEAKY.** No method x path combination refits varsel inside CV folds. The pattern is universal: varsel runs once on the full preprocessed calibration matrix with full y, produces a frozen `top_indices` array, and `_run_single_config()` / `run_one_class_cv()` / `cross_val_score()` receives the pre-subsetted data with no per-fold varsel re-run.
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:72:The Refinement/Model Development path is N/A for all methods because it reuses the saved wavelength list from the search result - it does not re-run any varsel algorithm.
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:77:   CARS is the most popular varsel method in chemometrics (adaptive reweighted sampling with MC subsampling). It uses y both for MC sampling stratification and for the internal PLS fitness evaluation. With N<200, full-data CARS inflates R2cv by 0.05-0.15 in noisy data. It appears in all four search paths (grid, old Bayesian, unified Bayesian, one-class grid) and is the default varsel method for the unified Bayesian path. Affects the headline metric every user sees.
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:80:   The Pipeline is fit on full `(X_for_models, y_np)` at line 2274. For PLS models, this calls `compute_vip()` on the full-data model (models.py:1786), which uses y-dependent PLS weights/scores. For tree models, `feature_importances_` come from a full-data fit. This is the fallback varsel method and runs for every model x preprocessing combination. Impact: moderate (VIP/feature_importances are less noisy than CARS, but still inflate R2cv by ~0.02-0.05 on small datasets).
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:83:   UVE prefilter runs on `(X_preprocessed, y_oc)` where `y_oc` is the +1/-1 inlier/outlier label vector derived from full data. This prefilter can eliminate 30-60% of variables before any per-method varsel runs, compounding the leakage. For one-class, the y labels are especially sensitive (binary inlier/outlier), so full-data UVE has a larger inflation effect than for regression. See T-04 overlap.
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:87:1. **Grid search path (highest user traffic)** - Create a `VarselTransformer` sklearn-compatible wrapper that wraps each varsel method and can be inserted into a Pipeline before the model. Extend `_run_single_config()` to optionally include the VarselTransformer in the pipeline so varsel refits per-fold from train indices only. This single fix covers the grid search path for all methods simultaneously. Reference: `cv_utils.py:cross_val_predict_pooled` as the per-fold loop infrastructure.
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:89:2. **Unified Bayesian path** - The `compute_importances()` function at `unified_bayesian.py:593` must be replaced with per-fold varsel inside the Optuna objective. This is architecturally harder than the grid fix because the objective already runs per-trial (not per-fold), and the importances are cached per preprocessing+method across trials. The fix: instead of caching importances computed on full data, cache per-fold varsel results keyed by `(preprocess, method, fold_train_indices_hash)`.
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:91:3. **Old Bayesian path (`bayesian_utils.py`)** - Same fix pattern as unified Bayesian but simpler (the old path calls `_run_single_config_fn` which could be upgraded to include varsel in the pipeline, piggybacking on the grid search fix).
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:95:5. **NSGA-II guidance (lowest priority)** - The CARS-Tree guidance at `nsga2_search.py:1872` is NOT leakage in the traditional sense (it biases the initial population sampling, not the CV evaluation). The CV evaluation uses the chromosome's wavelength mask directly with no y-using varsel. Mark as LOW priority.

.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:219:- **Grid search**: LEAKY. `cars_selection(X_transformed_varsel, y_np, ...)` at `search.py:2552-2563`. MC sampling + exponential decay + internal PLS all use full y. Frozen `top_indices` at 2829. **Magnitude**: High. CARS's adaptive reweighted sampling is highly sensitive to which samples are included. Full-data CARS on N<200 typically inflates R2cv by 0.05-0.15. See Top 3 above. **Fix**: VarselTransformer wrapper.
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:221:- **Unified Bayesian**: LEAKY. `cars_selection(X, y, ...)` at `unified_bayesian.py:655-665`. Importances cached per `(preprocess, method, model)` at 990, then `top_indices` at 992. **Fix**: Per-fold varsel inside Optuna objective.
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:222:- **NSGA-II**: LEAKY (guidance only, LOW priority). `cars_selection(X, y_for_importance, ...)` at `nsga2_search.py:1872`. Called once outside `_evaluate()`. Result biases initial population sampling probability but does NOT directly determine the wavelength mask used in CV. The chromosome encodes its own mask and CV runs on that mask via `cross_val_score` at ~1450. **Magnitude**: Low. The CARS guidance inflates the probability that good wavelengths appear in the initial population, but the CV score itself is computed on the chromosome's mask without varsel. The inflation is indirect and small (~0.01-0.02). **Fix**: Use only unsupervised initialization (uniform random) or accept the bias. Lowest priority.
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:243:- **Grid search**: LEAKY. `vcpa_iriv(X_transformed_varsel, y_np, ...)` at `search.py:2647-2654`. Iterative elimination with binary matrix sampling uses full y. **Magnitude**: Moderate-high. VCPA's nested outer/inner iterations are computationally expensive, and full-data selection amplifies noise. **Fix**: VarselTransformer wrapper.
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:251:- **Grid search**: LEAKY. `ga_pls_selection(X_transformed_varsel, y_np, ...)` at `search.py:2696-2707` (linear models) and `search.py:2726-2737` (default). GA fitness evaluation uses PLS CV on full y. **Magnitude**: High. GA-PLS explores many variable subsets with full-data fitness, so the selected variables are tuned to the full dataset. **Fix**: VarselTransformer wrapper.
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:259:- **Grid search**: LEAKY. `ga_lightgbm_selection(X_transformed_varsel, y_np, ...)` at `search.py:2710-2722` (tree models). Same GA pattern with LightGBM fitness. **Fix**: VarselTransformer wrapper.
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:267:- **Grid search**: LEAKY. Pipeline fit on full `(X_for_models, y_np)` at `search.py:2274`. `get_feature_importances(fitted_model, ...)` at `search.py:2477-2478` extracts importances from the full-data model. For PLS: VIP scores (`models.py:1786` calls `compute_vip()`). For tree models: `feature_importances_`. For linear: `|coef_|`. All y-dependent. **Magnitude**: Moderate. See Top 3 above. **Fix**: The model must be fit per-fold; importances extracted from per-fold models. This is the hardest to fix because it requires per-fold model fitting before variable ranking, creating a chicken-and-egg problem. Practical approach: use an initial full-spectrum per-fold fit to rank variables, then refit the actual model on the selected subset per-fold.
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:275:- **Grid search**: N/A. Grid search does not use region-based varsel (it uses explicit varsel methods from the dropdown).

.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:282:### VIP (`compute_vip` in models.py)
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:283:- **Grid search**: LEAKY (via `importance` mode). VIP is computed inside `get_feature_importances()` at `models.py:1784-1786` when `model_name` is PLS/PLS-DA. Frozen mask applied to all subsequent varsel methods. **Magnitude**: Compounds with whatever varsel method runs next. If UVE prefilter eliminates 40% of variables, the downstream varsel works on a pre-filtered space that was selected using full y.
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:319:1. **`bayesian_utils.py:261` hardcodes `random_state=42` inside varsel calls** - Line 442, 455, 469, 488, 502, 520. This ignores the user's `random_state` setting and makes Bayesian varsel non-reproducible across runs if the user changes the random seed. Grid search correctly threads `random_state` through.
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:332:- **CV Strategy Phase 2** (from PROJECT_STATUS follow-ups) - proposes propagating outer CV strategy into varsel inner loops. This is complementary but independent: even if inner CV strategy matches outer, the leakage persists because varsel runs on full data before outer CV starts.
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:348:(a) **Pipeline approach**: `Pipeline([("varsel", VarselTransformer(method, params)), ("model", model)])` - sklearn handles per-fold refitting automatically. Best for grid search and old Bayesian (which already use sklearn pipelines).
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:350:(b) **Manual per-fold loop**: In the CV loop, for each fold, run varsel on `(X_train, y_train)` only, then evaluate on `X_test`. Necessary for methods that don't fit sklearn's `fit/transform` API cleanly (e.g., iPLS which returns subsets, not a transform).
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:352:(c) **Hybrid**: For Bayesian paths, run varsel inside the Optuna objective per-trial on full data (still leaky per-trial, but at least different subsets per hyperparameter configuration). This is the minimal-effort improvement - it doesn't fix the per-fold leakage but reduces the compounding effect.
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:354:Option (a) is recommended for the grid search fix. It requires implementing ~15 VarselTransformer classes (one per method), each wrapping the existing varsel function into `fit()` (runs varsel on train data) and `transform()` (selects the top-N variables). The existing `variable_selection.py` functions remain unchanged; the transformers call them.

.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:492:**Architecture:** Build a `VarselTransformer` family of sklearn-compatible wrappers that run varsel methods inside CV folds via Pipeline composition. Refactor each search path to use these Pipelines.
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:504:The T-01 audit identified 49 LEAKY methodxpath combinations in the `spectral_predict` library. Every y-using variable-selection method computes importances once on the full calibration matrix `(X_preprocessed, y)`, produces a frozen `top_indices` array, and passes pre-subsetted data into cross-validation. CV never re-runs varsel per fold, so every fold tests on data whose variable subset was chosen using that fold's test rows. Empirically this inflates Rýcv by **0.02 to 0.15**, with the largest inflations on small N (ó 200) and aggressive methods (CARS, GA-PLS, UVE-CARS-SPA).
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:506:The fix is the canonical sklearn pattern: wrap each varsel method in a `TransformerMixin` and compose with the model in a `Pipeline`. Under this architecture, `cross_val_score`, `cross_val_predict`, and the project's `cross_val_predict_pooled` automatically refit varsel per fold on training data only.
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:515:X, y  varsel(X, y)  top_indices  X[:, top_indices]  CV(model)  Rýcv (inflated)
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:521:X, y  CV(Pipeline([varsel_transformer, model]))  Rýcv (honest)
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:523:   X_train, y_train  varsel(X_train, y_train)  top_indices_train
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:533:| Model-importance | Importances come from the **fitted model** (VIP, `feature_importances_`, `\|coef_\|`) | `importance` mode | `ModelImportanceVarselTransformer` |
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:539:The existing caching architecture (`_varsel_cache` at `search.py:1896`, `importance_cache` in `unified_bayesian.py`) caches importances keyed by `(preprocess_cfg, varsel_method, model_name)` and reuses them across all hyperparameter combinations and all CV folds. This is incompatible with per-fold varsel.
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:543:- **Wrapped methods (SPA, UVE, CARS, .):** No caching at the search.py level. `VarselTransformer.fit()` calls the varsel function directly. Per-fold runtime is acceptable because fold-train data is smaller (~80 % of full-data runtime).
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:546:- **Old Bayesian (`bayesian_utils.py`):** Remove `_varsel_cache` entirely. Each Optuna trial's `_run_single_config_fn` constructs a fresh Pipeline.
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:570:The four transformer classes live in a new module `src/spectral_predict/varsel_transformer.py`. Each is a fully sklearn-compatible estimator with a `fit(X, y) -> self`, `transform(X) -> X[:, selected_indices_]`, and `get_support(indices=False)` API. They do not cache anything at module level.

.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:892:The `importance` mode is harder than wrapped methods because importances come from the **fitted model itself** (PLS VIP, tree `feature_importances_`, linear `|coef_|`). .\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:1587:The `importance` mode is special. Whereas wrapped methods use a fixed varsel algorithm independent of the model, `importance` mode extracts importances from the **fitted model itself**. Different `(alpha, n_components, num_leaves, ...)` settings produce different feature rankings on the same training data. The cache key would have to include all model hyperparameters AND all fold-train indices, which collapses to "compute every time."

.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:5365:docs\plans\2026-04-29-varsel-leakage-fix.md:19:The T-01 audit identified 49 LEAKY method×path combinations in the `spectral_predict` library. Every y-using variable-selection method computes importances once on the full calibration matrix `(X_preprocessed, y)`, produces a frozen `top_indices` array, and passes pre-subsetted data into cross-validation. CV never re-runs varsel per fold, so every fold tests on data whose variable subset was chosen using that fold's test rows. Empirically this inflates R²cv by **0.02 to 0.15**, with the largest inflations on small N (≤ 200) and aggressive methods (CARS, GA-PLS, UVE-CARS-SPA).

.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:5413:docs\plans\2026-04-29-varsel-leakage-fix.md:407:The `importance` mode is harder than wrapped methods because importances come from the **fitted model itself** (PLS VIP, tree `feature_importances_`, linear `|coef_|`). Per-fold caching is impossible because different hyperparameters produce different importances on the same training data, so a per-fold *and* per-hyperparam computation is required. The transformer clones the model, fits on `(X_train, y_train)`, extracts importances via `get_feature_importances()`, picks top-N indices, then the downstream model in the Pipeline refits on the selected subset.

.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:5423:docs\plans\2026-04-29-varsel-leakage-fix.md:546:git commit -m "feat: ModelImportanceVarselTransformer for importance-mode varsel"
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:5424:docs\plans\2026-04-29-varsel-leakage-fix.md:551:These methods do not return per-feature importances; they return a list of candidate **subsets** with associated RMSECV/score from an inner CV. The transformer picks the best subset by the method's own inner-CV result. Different outer folds may legitimately pick different subsets - that is correct behaviour under nested CV and a key change from the leaky single-shot version.
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:5425:docs\plans\2026-04-29-varsel-leakage-fix.md:718:The one-class path currently runs UVE prefilter + LightGBM surrogate importances on the full encoded `(X, y_oc)` once. Both steps must move per-fold. The transformer encodes outlier labels +1/-1 from `y_oc_train`, runs UVE on `(X_train, y_oc_train_encoded)` for the prefilter mask, then calls `compute_one_class_importances` on `(X_train[:, prefilter_mask], y_oc_train_encoded)` to obtain LightGBM surrogate importances and pick top-N.

.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:5856:1. The `VarselTransformer + Pipeline` pattern is architecturally sound for normal grid/Bayesian regression/classification paths. It does fix leakage when the estimator passed into `cross_val_predict_pooled` owns both varsel and model fitting. Keep that core direction.
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:5859:4. Caching per `(preprocess, method, model, fold_train_indices_hash)` is not pointless, but its usefulness is narrow. It helps only across repeated trials with identical varsel params and identical train folds. For importance mode, hyperparams must be in the key, so it mostly collapses to compute-every-time. Removing cross-fold/full-data caches is the right default.

The audit calls out [create_region_subsets](C:/Users/sponheim/git/dasp/.worktrees/varsel-leakage-fix/src/spectral_predict/regions.py:138) as y-using, and current callers precompute/cache regions in [bayesian_utils.py](C:/Users/sponheim/git/dasp/.worktrees/varsel-leakage-fix/src/spectral_predict/bayesian_utils.py:263) and [unified_bayesian.py](C:/Users/sponheim/git/dasp/.worktrees/varsel-leakage-fix/src/spectral_predict/unified_bayesian.py:1077). .\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:5882:4. One-class label handling is underspecified. The plan says `y_oc_train`, but the current CV semantics train on fold-train inliers and test on held-out inliers plus all outliers. The implementation must explicitly define the varsel calibration set per fold, likely `train_inliers + train/outlier policy`, and must not let held-out inlier labels or repeated test outliers drive UVE/LightGBM selection.
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:5883:5. The dispatcher code is too speculative for a 20-task implementation plan. Examples: `ga_lightgbm` is routed through `ga_pls_selection` despite a real [ga_lightgbm_selection](C:/Users/sponheim/git/dasp/.worktrees/varsel-leakage-fix/src/spectral_predict/ga_lightgbm.py:522); `SubsetVarselTransformer` passes `pls_components` to functions like [ipls_forward](C:/Users/sponheim/git/dasp/.worktrees/varsel-leakage-fix/src/spectral_predict/variable_selection.py:1727) that do not accept it; and `_as_importance_vector` silently falls back to uniform importances. Silent fallback here would hide broken varsel rather than fix leakage.
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:5886:1. The `VarselTransformer + Pipeline` pattern is architecturally sound for normal grid/Bayesian regression/classification paths. It does fix leakage when the estimator passed into `cross_val_predict_pooled` owns both varsel and model fitting. Keep that core direction.
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:5889:4. Caching per `(preprocess, method, model, fold_train_indices_hash)` is not pointless, but its usefulness is narrow. It helps only across repeated trials with identical varsel params and identical train folds. For importance mode, hyperparams must be in the key, so it mostly collapses to compute-every-time. Removing cross-fold/full-data caches is the right default.
.\codex_review_varsel_plan.txt:5891:6. Existing tests “still pass” is plausible only after careful result-contract preservation. Most likely regressions are result dict fields around `subset_indices`, `top_wavelengths`, `n_vars`, PLS component pruning after varsel, and one-class metrics/reporting.

.\docs\UserGuide.md:2057:### Variable Importance in Projection (VIP)
.\docs\UserGuide.md:2059:VIP scores quantify each variable's contribution to the PLS model:
.\docs\UserGuide.md:2061:$$VIP_j = \sqrt{p \sum_{a=1}^{A} \frac{w_{aj}^2 \cdot SSY_a}{SSY_{total}}}$$
.\docs\UserGuide.md:2070:- VIP > 1: Variable is important for the model
.\docs\UserGuide.md:2071:- VIP < 0.8: Variable may be a candidate for removal
.\docs\UserGuide.md:2072:- VIP = 1: Average contribution
.\docs\UserGuide.md:2082:- VIP scores enable variable selection
.\docs\UserGuide.md:2101:4. **Wavelength selection**: Use VIP scores to identify important spectral regions
.\docs\UserGuide.md:2194:- VIP scores identify discriminating wavelengths
.\docs\UserGuide.md:2381:4. **Comparison to VIP**: Different selection mechanisms; compare results
.\docs\UserGuide.md:3219:| Feature selection | Lasso, ElasticNet | VIP (PLS) |
.\docs\UserGuide.md:5268:#### 1.1.1 VIP (Variable Importance in Projection) for PLS
.\docs\UserGuide.md:5272:VIP scores quantify each variable's contribution to the PLS model by measuring how much the variable participates in explaining both the predictor (X) and response (Y) variance.
.\docs\UserGuide.md:5276:For a PLS model with $A$ components, the VIP score for variable $j$ is:
.\docs\UserGuide.md:5278:$$VIP_j = \sqrt{\frac{p \sum_{a=1}^{A} SS_{Y,a} \cdot w_{ja}^2}{\sum_{a=1}^{A} SS_{Y,a}}}$$
.\docs\UserGuide.md:5297:   b. VIP[j] = sqrt(p * weighted_sum / total_SS_Y)
.\docs\UserGuide.md:5298:7. Return VIP scores
.\docs\UserGuide.md:5302:- VIP > 1.0: Variable is highly important (rule of thumb threshold)
.\docs\UserGuide.md:5303:- VIP between 0.8-1.0: Moderately important
.\docs\UserGuide.md:5304:- VIP < 0.8: Less important, candidate for elimination
.\docs\UserGuide.md:5309:def compute_vip(pls_model, X, y):
.\docs\UserGuide.md:5318:    # VIP calculation (vectorized)
.\docs\UserGuide.md:5321:    vip_scores = np.sqrt(n_features * weight / ssy_total)
.\docs\UserGuide.md:5323:    return vip_scores
.\docs\UserGuide.md:5445:| **Threshold-Based** | Select variables where importance > threshold | When natural grouping exists (e.g., VIP > 1.0) |
.\docs\UserGuide.md:6330:| **VIP/Top-N** | Importance | Yes | No | Partial | Fast | Fixed N |
.\docs\UserGuide.md:6411:- Selection frequency indicates variable importance reliability
.\docs\UserGuide.md:6433:| **VIP** | Variable Importance in Projection |
.\docs\UserGuide.md:9947:Measure of how much each feature (wavelength) contributes to model predictions. Methods include VIP scores (PLS), coefficient magnitude (linear models), and gain-based importance (tree models).
.\docs\UserGuide.md:10214:Variable selection method comparing real variable importance to artificial noise variables.

.\docs\T01_VARSEL_LEAKAGE_AUDIT.md:4:> Scope: Determine whether each variable selection method x search path refits varsel inside CV folds (CLEAN) or computes once on full calibration data with a frozen list passed into CV (LEAKY).
.\docs\T01_VARSEL_LEAKAGE_AUDIT.md:18:**Every y-using variable selection method in every search path it actually runs in is LEAKY.** No method x path combination refits varsel inside CV folds. The pattern is universal: varsel runs once on the full preprocessed calibration matrix with full y, produces a frozen `top_indices` array, and `_run_single_config()` / `run_one_class_cv()` / `cross_val_score()` receives the pre-subsetted data with no per-fold varsel re-run.
.\docs\T01_VARSEL_LEAKAGE_AUDIT.md:20:The Refinement/Model Development path is N/A for all methods because it reuses the saved wavelength list from the search result — it does not re-run any varsel algorithm.
.\docs\T01_VARSEL_LEAKAGE_AUDIT.md:25:   CARS is the most popular varsel method in chemometrics (adaptive reweighted sampling with MC subsampling). It uses y both for MC sampling stratification and for the internal PLS fitness evaluation. With N<200, full-data CARS inflates R2cv by 0.05-0.15 in noisy data. It appears in all four search paths (grid, old Bayesian, unified Bayesian, one-class grid) and is the default varsel method for the unified Bayesian path. Affects the headline metric every user sees.
.\docs\T01_VARSEL_LEAKAGE_AUDIT.md:28:   The Pipeline is fit on full `(X_for_models, y_np)` at line 2274. For PLS models, this calls `compute_vip()` on the full-data model (models.py:1786), which uses y-dependent PLS weights/scores. For tree models, `feature_importances_` come from a full-data fit. This is the fallback varsel method and runs for every model x preprocessing combination. Impact: moderate (VIP/feature_importances are less noisy than CARS, but still inflate R2cv by ~0.02-0.05 on small datasets).
.\docs\T01_VARSEL_LEAKAGE_AUDIT.md:31:   UVE prefilter runs on `(X_preprocessed, y_oc)` where `y_oc` is the +1/-1 inlier/outlier label vector derived from full data. This prefilter can eliminate 30-60% of variables before any per-method varsel runs, compounding the leakage. For one-class, the y labels are especially sensitive (binary inlier/outlier), so full-data UVE has a larger inflation effect than for regression. See T-04 overlap.
.\docs\T01_VARSEL_LEAKAGE_AUDIT.md:35:1. **Grid search path (highest user traffic)** — Create a `VarselTransformer` sklearn-compatible wrapper that wraps each varsel method and can be inserted into a Pipeline before the model. Extend `_run_single_config()` to optionally include the VarselTransformer in the pipeline so varsel refits per-fold from train indices only. This single fix covers the grid search path for all methods simultaneously. Reference: `cv_utils.py:cross_val_predict_pooled` as the per-fold loop infrastructure.
.\docs\T01_VARSEL_LEAKAGE_AUDIT.md:37:2. **Unified Bayesian path** — The `compute_importances()` function at `unified_bayesian.py:593` must be replaced with per-fold varsel inside the Optuna objective. This is architecturally harder than the grid fix because the objective already runs per-trial (not per-fold), and the importances are cached per preprocessing+method across trials. The fix: instead of caching importances computed on full data, cache per-fold varsel results keyed by `(preprocess, method, fold_train_indices_hash)`.
.\docs\T01_VARSEL_LEAKAGE_AUDIT.md:39:3. **Old Bayesian path (`bayesian_utils.py`)** — Same fix pattern as unified Bayesian but simpler (the old path calls `_run_single_config_fn` which could be upgraded to include varsel in the pipeline, piggybacking on the grid search fix).
.\docs\T01_VARSEL_LEAKAGE_AUDIT.md:43:5. **NSGA-II guidance (lowest priority)** — The CARS-Tree guidance at `nsga2_search.py:1872` is NOT leakage in the traditional sense (it biases the initial population sampling, not the CV evaluation). The CV evaluation uses the chromosome's wavelength mask directly with no y-using varsel. Mark as LOW priority.

.\docs\T01_VARSEL_LEAKAGE_AUDIT.md:215:- **Grid search**: LEAKY. This is the hardest to fix because it requires per-fold model fitting before variable ranking, creating a chicken-and-egg problem. Practical approach: use an initial full-spectrum per-fold fit to rank variables, then refit the actual model on the selected subset per-fold.

.\docs\T01_VARSEL_LEAKAGE_AUDIT.md:231:- **Grid search**: LEAKY (via `importance` mode). VIP is computed inside `get_feature_importances()` at `models.py:1784-1786` when `model_name` is PLS/PLS-DA. .\docs\T01_VARSEL_LEAKAGE_AUDIT.md:255:The UVE prefilter is a separate leakage vector that runs before the per-method varsel loop.
.\docs\T01_VARSEL_LEAKAGE_AUDIT.md:257:- **Grid search**: LEAKY. `get_uve_threshold(X_transformed_varsel, y_np, ...)` at `search.py:2289-2305`. Reduces the variable set before the varsel loop at 2314. Frozen mask applied to all subsequent varsel methods. **Magnitude**: Compounds with whatever varsel method runs next. If UVE prefilter eliminates 40% of variables, the downstream varsel works on a pre-filtered space that was selected using full y.
.\docs\T01_VARSEL_LEAKAGE_AUDIT.md:267:1. **`bayesian_utils.py:261` hardcodes `random_state=42` inside varsel calls** — Line 442, 455, 469, 488, 502, 520. This ignores the user's `random_state` setting and makes Bayesian varsel non-reproducible across runs if the user changes the random seed. Grid search correctly threads `random_state` through.
.\docs\T01_VARSEL_LEAKAGE_AUDIT.md:280:- **CV Strategy Phase 2** (from PROJECT_STATUS follow-ups) — proposes propagating outer CV strategy into varsel inner loops. This is complementary but independent: even if inner CV strategy matches outer, the leakage persists because varsel runs on full data before outer CV starts.
.\docs\T01_VARSEL_LEAKAGE_AUDIT.md:296:(a) **Pipeline approach**: `Pipeline([("varsel", VarselTransformer(method, params)), ("model", model)])` — sklearn handles per-fold refitting automatically. Best for grid search and old Bayesian (which already use sklearn pipelines).
.\docs\T01_VARSEL_LEAKAGE_AUDIT.md:298:(b) **Manual per-fold loop**: In the CV loop, for each fold, run varsel on `(X_train, y_train)` only, then evaluate on `X_test`. Necessary for methods that don't fit sklearn's `fit/transform` API cleanly (e.g., iPLS which returns subsets, not a transform).
.\docs\T01_VARSEL_LEAKAGE_AUDIT.md:300:(c) **Hybrid**: For Bayesian paths, run varsel inside the Optuna objective per-trial on full data (still leaky per-trial, but at least different subsets per hyperparameter configuration). This is the minimal-effort improvement — it doesn't fix the per-fold leakage but reduces the compounding effect.
.\docs\T01_VARSEL_LEAKAGE_AUDIT.md:302:Option (a) is recommended for the grid search fix. It requires implementing ~15 VarselTransformer classes (one per method), each wrapping the existing varsel function into `fit()` (runs varsel on train data) and `transform()` (selects the top-N variables). The existing `variable_selection.py` functions remain unchanged; the transformers call them.
.\docs\SESSION_LOG_ARCHIVE.md:289:1. **`search.py:5698-5703` — `all_vars`/`top_vars` swap for grid-search varsel rows.** Was storing the pre-subset working set (`wavelengths_current`) in `all_vars` and the selected subset (`wavelengths_subset`) in `top_vars`. Downstream consumers (`spectral_predict_gui_optimized.py:30556-30573` Model-Dev reload, `contamination.py:972-989` validation rebuild) read `all_vars` as "the trained wavelength list", so a variable-selected grid-search one-class model would be reconstructed on the full spectrum. Fixed: both fields now hold the selected subset, matching the Bayesian contract at `unified_bayesian.py:1046-1050`.
.\docs\SESSION_LOG_ARCHIVE.md:481:**Fix:** Added `scaler`, `pca_reducer`, `oc_score_stats` to both result dict blocks (full-spectrum and varsel).
.\docs\SESSION_LOG.md:11:### NSGA-II CARS guidance is NOT per-fold varsel
.\docs\SESSION_LOG.md:12:`nsga2_search.py:1872` calls `cars_selection(X, y, ...)` but this is OUTSIDE `_evaluate()`. The result biases the initial population's wavelength sampling probability only — the per-individual CV at `_evaluate():1310` uses the chromosome's own wavelength mask with `cross_val_score`. So NSGA-II's CARS is not per-fold varsel leakage in the traditional sense; it's population-initialization bias. Low priority.
.\docs\SESSION_LOG.md:14:### Unified Bayesian has only 3-4 varsel methods, not the full set
.\docs\SESSION_LOG.md:18:`_run_refined_model_thread()` in the GUI (line 35273) parses wavelengths from the saved result row's `all_vars` field or from `_original_wavelength_order`. It does NOT re-run any varsel algorithm. So the refinement path inherits whatever leakage the search path had, but doesn't introduce new leakage.

.\docs\RECONCILED_ROADMAP_2026-04-29.md:54:**Why:** Uses `np.var(y)` instead of per-component `y_loadings_`. Wavelength rankings via VIP are skewed.
.\docs\RECONCILED_ROADMAP_2026-04-29.md:399:**T-01: the audit nobody did.** All three reviewers agreed the analyses kept saying "audit per-fold variable selection" but never did the audit. CARS, UVE, VIP, SPA can leak information if computed on full data before CV. By chemometrics convention, per-spectrum operations like SNV and Savitzky-Golay derivatives are NOT leakage (your domain rule, now in saved memory). But variable selection that uses your y-values is real leakage, and we don't know which paths in dasp do it correctly. T-01 finds out.

.\docs\section7_advanced.md:1154:EstimatedEPO and OPLS-DA use multivariate decomposition (PCA or PLS) to separate the *systematic* contamination signal from noise. Their influence curves are computed from squared loadings (EPO) or VIP scores (OPLS-DA), which concentrate influence at wavelengths where the contaminant has a *structured, repeatable* effect.

.\docs\section5_variable_selection.md:23:#### 1.1.1 VIP (Variable Importance in Projection) for PLS
.\docs\section5_variable_selection.md:27:VIP scores quantify each variable's contribution to the PLS model by measuring how much the variable participates in explaining both the predictor (X) and response (Y) variance.
.\docs\section5_variable_selection.md:31:For a PLS model with $A$ components, the VIP score for variable $j$ is:
.\docs\section5_variable_selection.md:33:$$VIP_j = \sqrt{\frac{p \sum_{a=1}^{A} SS_{Y,a} \cdot w_{ja}^2}{\sum_{a=1}^{A} SS_{Y,a}}}$$
.\docs\section5_variable_selection.md:52:   b. VIP[j] = sqrt(p * weighted_sum / total_SS_Y)
.\docs\section5_variable_selection.md:53:7. Return VIP scores
.\docs\section5_variable_selection.md:57:- VIP > 1.0: Variable is highly important (rule of thumb threshold)
.\docs\section5_variable_selection.md:58:- VIP between 0.8-1.0: Moderately important
.\docs\section5_variable_selection.md:59:- VIP < 0.8: Less important, candidate for elimination
.\docs\section5_variable_selection.md:64:def compute_vip(pls_model, X, y):
.\docs\section5_variable_selection.md:73:    # VIP calculation (vectorized)
.\docs\section5_variable_selection.md:76:    vip_scores = np.sqrt(n_features * weight / ssy_total)
.\docs\section5_variable_selection.md:78:    return vip_scores

.\docs\plans\2026-04-29-varsel-leakage-fix.md:7:**Architecture:** Build a `VarselTransformer` family of sklearn-compatible wrappers that run varsel methods inside CV folds via Pipeline composition. Refactor each search path to use these Pipelines.
.\docs\plans\2026-04-29-varsel-leakage-fix.md:19:The T-01 audit identified 49 LEAKY method×path combinations in the `spectral_predict` library. Every y-using variable-selection method computes importances once on the full calibration matrix `(X_preprocessed, y)`, produces a frozen `top_indices` array, and passes pre-subsetted data into cross-validation. CV never re-runs varsel per fold, so every fold tests on data whose variable subset was chosen using that fold's test rows. Empirically this inflates R²cv by **0.02 to 0.15**, with the largest inflations on small N (≤ 200) and aggressive methods (CARS, GA-PLS, UVE-CARS-SPA).
.\docs\plans\2026-04-29-varsel-leakage-fix.md:21:The fix is the canonical sklearn pattern: wrap each varsel method in a `TransformerMixin` and compose with the model in a `Pipeline`. Under this architecture, `cross_val_score`, `cross_val_predict`, and the project's `cross_val_predict_pooled` automatically refit varsel per fold on training data only.
.\docs\plans\2026-04-29-varsel-leakage-fix.md:30:X, y → varsel(X, y) → top_indices → X[:, top_indices] → CV(model) → R²cv (inflated)
.\docs\plans\2026-04-29-varsel-leakage-fix.md:36:X, y → CV(Pipeline([varsel_transformer, model])) → R²cv (honest)
.\docs\plans\2026-04-29-varsel-leakage-fix.md:38:   X_train, y_train → varsel(X_train, y_train) → top_indices_train
.\docs\plans\2026-04-29-varsel-leakage-fix.md:48:| Model-importance | Importances come from the **fitted model** (VIP, `feature_importances_`, `\|coef_\|`) | `importance` mode | `ModelImportanceVarselTransformer` |
.\docs\plans\2026-04-29-varsel-leakage-fix.md:54:The existing caching architecture (`_varsel_cache` at `search.py:1896`, `importance_cache` in `unified_bayesian.py`) caches importances keyed by `(preprocess_cfg, varsel_method, model_name)` and reuses them across all hyperparameter combinations and all CV folds. This is incompatible with per-fold varsel.
.\docs\plans\2026-04-29-varsel-leakage-fix.md:58:- **Wrapped methods (SPA, UVE, CARS, …):** No caching at the search.py level. `VarselTransformer.fit()` calls the varsel function directly. Per-fold runtime is acceptable because fold-train data is smaller (~80 % of full-data runtime).
.\docs\plans\2026-04-29-varsel-leakage-fix.md:61:- **Old Bayesian (`bayesian_utils.py`):** Remove `_varsel_cache` entirely. Each Optuna trial's `_run_single_config_fn` constructs a fresh Pipeline.
.\docs\plans\2026-04-29-varsel-leakage-fix.md:85:The four transformer classes live in a new module `src/spectral_predict/varsel_transformer.py`. Each is a fully sklearn-compatible estimator with a `fit(X, y) -> self`, `transform(X) -> X[:, selected_indices_]`, and `get_support(indices=False)` API. They do not cache anything at module level.
.\docs\plans\2026-04-29-varsel-leakage-fix.md:87:### Task 1.1: VarselTransformer base class with `_dispatch_varsel` for importance-returning methods
.\docs\plans\2026-04-29-varsel-leakage-fix.md:90:- Create: `src/spectral_predict/varsel_transformer.py`
.\docs\plans\2026-04-29-varsel-leakage-fix.md:91:- Test: `tests/test_varsel_transformer.py`
.\docs\plans\2026-04-29-varsel-leakage-fix.md:96:# tests/test_varsel_transformer.py

.\docs\plans\2026-04-29-varsel-leakage-fix.md:407:The `importance` mode is harder than wrapped methods because importances come from the **fitted model itself** (PLS VIP, tree `feature_importances_`, linear `|coef_|`). Per-fold caching is impossible because different hyperparameters produce different importances on the same training data, so a per-fold *and* per-hyperparam computation is required. The transformer clones the model, fits on `(X_train, y_train)`, extracts importances via `get_feature_importances()`, picks top-N indices, then the downstream model in the Pipeline refits on the selected subset.

.\docs\plans\2026-04-29-varsel-leakage-fix.md:1102:The `importance` mode is special. The cache key would have to include all model hyperparameters AND all fold-train indices, which collapses to "compute every time."

 1708: 
 1709: def compute_vip(pls_model, X, y):
 1710:     """
 1711:     Compute Variable Importance in Projection (VIP) scores for a fitted PLS model.
 1712: 
 1713:     Parameters
 1714:     ----------
 1715:     pls_model : PLSRegression or PLSTransformer
 1716:         Fitted PLS model
 1717:     X : array-like
 1718:         Training data
 1719:     y : array-like
 1720:         Target values
 1721: 
 1722:     Returns
 1723:     -------
 1724:     vip_scores : ndarray
 1725:         VIP score for each variable
 1726:     """
 1727:     # Handle PLSTransformer wrapper
 1728:     if isinstance(pls_model, PLSTransformer):
 1729:         pls_model = pls_model.pls_
 1730: 
 1731:     # Get PLS components
 1732:     W = pls_model.x_weights_  # (n_features, n_components)
 1733:     T = pls_model.x_scores_  # (n_samples, n_components)
 1734: 
 1735:     # Get explained variance by each component
 1736:     # SSY: sum of squares of y explained by each component
 1737:     y = np.asarray(y).reshape(-1, 1)
 1738:     ssy_comp = np.sum(T**2, axis=0) * np.var(y, axis=0)
 1739: 
 1740:     # Total SSY
 1741:     ssy_total = np.sum(ssy_comp)
 1742: 
 1743:     # VIP calculation (vectorized for performance)
 1744:     n_features = W.shape[0]
 1745:     n_components = W.shape[1]
 1746: 
 1747:     # Vectorized version: same math, but uses broadcasting instead of loop
 1748:     weight = np.sum((W ** 2) * ssy_comp, axis=1)  # Sum over components for each feature
 1749:     vip_scores = np.sqrt(n_features * weight / ssy_total)
 1750: 
 1751:     return vip_scores
 1752: 
 1753: 
 1754: def get_feature_importances(model, model_name, X, y):
 1755:     """
 1756:     Extract feature importances from a fitted model.
 1757: 
 1758:     Parameters
 1759:     ----------
 1760:     model : estimator
 1761:         Fitted model
 1762:     model_name : str
 1763:         Model type name
 1764:     X : array-like
 1765:         Training data
 1766:     y : array-like
 1767:         Target values
 1768: 
 1769:     Returns
 1770:     -------
 1771:     importances : ndarray
 1772:         Feature importance scores (higher = more important)
 1773:     """
 1774:     # Unwrap sklearn Pipeline to get the actual estimator
 1775:     # Models with StandardScaler preprocessing are stored as Pipeline([scaler, model])
 1776:     if isinstance(model, Pipeline):
 1777:         if 'model' in model.named_steps:
 1778:             model = model.named_steps['model']
 1779:         elif model_name == "PLS-DA" and 'pls' in model.named_steps:
 1780:             model = model.named_steps['pls']
 1781:         else:
 1782:             model = model.steps[-1][1]
 1783: 
 1784:     if model_name in ["PLS", "PLS-DA"]:
 1785:         # Use VIP scores
 1786:         return compute_vip(model, X, y)
 1787: 
 1788:     elif model_name in ["Ridge", "Lasso", "ElasticNet"]:
 1789:         # Get coefficients (linear models)
 1790:         coefs = np.abs(model.coef_)
 1791:         if len(coefs.shape) > 1:
 1792:             coefs = coefs[0]  # Handle multi-output case
 1793: 
 1794:         # Return absolute coefficient values as importance
 1795:         return coefs

# T-05: Fix VIP Formula Implementation Plan

> **For agentic workers:** REQUIRED SUB-SKILL: Use superpowers:subagent-driven-development or superpowers:executing-plans to implement this plan task-by-task. Steps use checkbox (`- [ ]`) syntax for tracking.

**Goal:** Replace the incorrect VIP computation in `compute_vip()` with the canonical Wold (2001) formula using per-component `y_loadings_`.

**Architecture:** Single-function fix in `src/spectral_predict/models.py:compute_vip`. Parametrized regression test verifies new formula matches independent computation; old formula does not.

**Tech Stack:** numpy, scikit-learn (PLSRegression), pytest, existing project test infrastructure.

**Source:** roadmap T-05 in `docs/RECONCILED_ROADMAP_2026-04-29.md`.

---

## Background

### The bug

`compute_vip()` (`src/spectral_predict/models.py:1709-1751`) currently computes per-component explained Y variance as:

```python
ssy_comp = np.sum(T**2, axis=0) * np.var(y, axis=0)   # WRONG
```

This multiplies the sum of squared X-scores for each component by a single scalar (`np.var(y)`), so every component gets weighted by the **same** value. The component-axis spread is therefore driven entirely by `sum(T_a**2)` (the X-score energy), independent of how much **Y** each component actually explains. VIP collapses to a re-weighting of `W**2` by X-score energy, which is *not* VIP.

### The canonical formula

The correct definition (Wold, 2001; Mehmood et al., 2012) is:

> Let `T` be the X-scores `(n_samples, A)`, `q` be the y-loadings `(A,)` (or `y_loadings_[:, 0]` for univariate Y in scikit-learn's `PLSRegression`), and `W` be the X-weights `(p, A)`.
>
> The Y variance explained by component `a` is:
>
>     SSY_a = q_a**2 * (T_a.T @ T_a)
>
> and the total explained Y variance is `SSY_total = sum_a SSY_a`.
>
> Then for variable `j`:
>
>     VIP_j = sqrt( p * sum_a [ SSY_a * (W_{j,a} / ||W_a||)**2 ] / SSY_total )

Notes:

- The `(W_{j,a} / ||W_a||)**2` term is the *normalized* squared X-weight for variable `j` on component `a`. With `sklearn.cross_decomposition.PLSRegression`, `x_weights_` columns are already unit-norm (`||W_a||==1`), so the normalization is a no-op for the sklearn case but we keep the explicit `/ ||W_a||**2` for correctness against any other PLS implementation that may pass through this function.
- `sum_j VIP_j**2 / p == 1` is the canonical sanity invariant ("average squared VIP equals 1") — used as a test.

### Why fix it now

VIP is exposed in the GUI's wavelength-importance ranking (`get_feature_importances` -> `compute_vip` for `model_name in ("PLS","PLS-DA")`) and feeds the `importance` variable-selection method. A skewed VIP shifts which wavelengths get selected, which propagates through the entire model search. Roadmap effort estimate: **30 min**.

### References

- Wold, S., Sjöström, M., Eriksson, L. (2001). "PLS-regression: a basic tool of chemometrics." *Chemometrics and Intelligent Laboratory Systems*, **58**(2), 109–130. https://doi.org/10.1016/S0169-7439(01)00155-1
- Mehmood, T., Liland, K. H., Snipen, L., Sæbø, S. (2012). "A review of variable selection methods in Partial Least Squares Regression." *Chemometrics and Intelligent Laboratory Systems*, **118**, 62–69. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.chemolab.2012.07.010 — see Eq. (1) for the VIP formula stated above.

## Task 1: Write failing regression test

**Goal:** Establish a test that fails on the *current* (buggy) implementation and will pass on the corrected one. The test exercises the numerical difference between the two formulas on a small synthetic PLS problem.

**Files:**

- Create: `tests/test_vip_formula.py`

- [ ] **Step 1.1:** Write the test file with the following exact contents.

  ```python
  """
  Regression test for the canonical VIP formula in compute_vip().

  Bug (pre-T-05): compute_vip() used np.var(y) as a per-component scalar weight
  for all components, so the per-component Y-explained-variance term
  collapsed to a constant times sum(T_a**2). This skewed VIP rankings on any
  PLS problem where Y-explained variance was not proportional to X-score
  energy (i.e. effectively all real PLS problems with > 1 component).

  Canonical formula (Wold 2001; Mehmood et al. 2012, Eq. 1):

      SSY_a = q_a**2 * (T_a.T @ T_a)               # q = y_loadings_
      VIP_j = sqrt( p * sum_a [ SSY_a * (W_{j,a} / ||W_a||)**2 ] / SSY_total )

  This test:
    1. Builds a small synthetic PLS regression problem where the OLD and NEW
       formulas disagree by > 1e-3 on at least one variable (proves the test
       is sensitive to the bug).
    2. Compares compute_vip() output to an independent reference
       implementation of the canonical formula.
    3. Verifies the canonical invariant: sum(VIP**2) / p ~= 1.
  """

  from __future__ import annotations

  import numpy as np
  import pytest
  from sklearn.cross_decomposition import PLSRegression

  from spectral_predict.models import compute_vip


  # ---------- helpers ----------

  def _reference_vip_canonical(pls, X, y):
      """Independent VIP reference using y_loadings_ (Wold 2001)."""
      W = np.asarray(pls.x_weights_)        # (p, A)
      T = np.asarray(pls.x_scores_)         # (n, A)
      Q = np.asarray(pls.y_loadings_)       # (n_targets, A) for sklearn >=1.1
      # univariate Y -> q is shape (1, A); squeeze to (A,)
      q = Q.ravel() if Q.ndim == 1 else Q[0, :]
      p = W.shape[0]

      # Per-component Y SS
      ssy_comp = (q ** 2) * np.sum(T ** 2, axis=0)   # (A,)
      ssy_total = ssy_comp.sum()

      # Normalized squared weights (sklearn x_weights_ cols are unit-norm,
      # but normalize defensively for any other caller).
      w_norm_sq = (W ** 2) / (np.sum(W ** 2, axis=0, keepdims=True) + 1e-300)

      vip = np.sqrt(p * (w_norm_sq @ ssy_comp) / (ssy_total + 1e-300))
      return vip


  def _reference_vip_OLD_BUGGY(pls, X, y):
      """The pre-T-05 formula. Used only to assert the test would catch it."""
      W = np.asarray(pls.x_weights_)
      T = np.asarray(pls.x_scores_)
      y_arr = np.asarray(y).reshape(-1, 1)
      ssy_comp = np.sum(T ** 2, axis=0) * np.var(y_arr, axis=0)
      ssy_total = np.sum(ssy_comp)
      p = W.shape[0]
      weight = np.sum((W ** 2) * ssy_comp, axis=1)
      return np.sqrt(p * weight / ssy_total)


  # ---------- fixtures ----------

  @pytest.fixture
  def synthetic_pls_problem():
      """
      Two latent factors with very different Y-loading magnitudes:
      - factor 1 contributes strongly to Y (q1 = 5.0)
      - factor 2 contributes weakly to Y (q2 = 0.5) but has comparable X energy
      The old (constant np.var(y)) formula will badly mis-weight factor 2.
      """
      rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
      n_samples = 60
      p = 20

      # Two orthogonal latent score vectors with similar X-energy
      t1 = rng.standard_normal(n_samples)
      t2 = rng.standard_normal(n_samples)
      # Two distinct loading patterns (peaks in different variables)
      w1 = np.zeros(p); w1[2:6] = 1.0
      w2 = np.zeros(p); w2[12:16] = 1.0
      X = np.outer(t1, w1) + np.outer(t2, w2) + 0.05 * rng.standard_normal((n_samples, p))

      # Y depends ~10x more on factor 1 than factor 2
      y = 5.0 * t1 + 0.5 * t2 + 0.05 * rng.standard_normal(n_samples)
      return X, y


  @pytest.fixture
  def fitted_pls(synthetic_pls_problem):
      X, y = synthetic_pls_problem
      pls = PLSRegression(n_components=2, scale=False)
      pls.fit(X, y)
      return pls, X, y


  # ---------- tests ----------

  class TestVIPCanonicalFormula:
      def test_old_and_new_formulas_disagree_on_this_problem(self, fitted_pls):
          """
          Sanity check that this synthetic problem is sensitive: the old and
          new formulas must produce materially different VIPs, otherwise the
          regression test below would be vacuous.
          """
          pls, X, y = fitted_pls
          old = _reference_vip_OLD_BUGGY(pls, X, y)
          new = _reference_vip_canonical(pls, X, y)
          max_abs_diff = float(np.max(np.abs(old - new)))
          assert max_abs_diff > 1e-3, (
              "Synthetic problem is not sensitive enough — old and new VIP "
              f"agree to {max_abs_diff:.2e}. Adjust the fixture."
          )

      def test_compute_vip_matches_canonical_reference(self, fitted_pls):
          """compute_vip() output equals an independent canonical implementation."""
          pls, X, y = fitted_pls
          got = compute_vip(pls, X, y)
          want = _reference_vip_canonical(pls, X, y)
          np.testing.assert_allclose(got, want, rtol=1e-10, atol=1e-12)

      def test_compute_vip_does_not_match_old_buggy_formula(self, fitted_pls):
          """compute_vip() must NOT reproduce the pre-T-05 buggy output."""
          pls, X, y = fitted_pls
          got = compute_vip(pls, X, y)
          old = _reference_vip_OLD_BUGGY(pls, X, y)
          max_abs_diff = float(np.max(np.abs(got - old)))
          assert max_abs_diff > 1e-3, (
              "compute_vip() still matches the old (buggy) formula — fix not applied."
          )

      def test_canonical_invariant_average_squared_vip_is_one(self, fitted_pls):
          """sum(VIP**2) / p == 1 is the textbook invariant (Wold 2001)."""
          pls, X, y = fitted_pls
          vip = compute_vip(pls, X, y)
          p = pls.x_weights_.shape[0]
          mean_sq = float(np.sum(vip ** 2) / p)
          assert mean_sq == pytest.approx(1.0, rel=1e-6, abs=1e-8)

      def test_output_shape_and_nonnegativity(self, fitted_pls):
          pls, X, y = fitted_pls
          vip = compute_vip(pls, X, y)
          assert vip.shape == (pls.x_weights_.shape[0],)
          assert np.all(vip >= 0)
          assert np.all(np.isfinite(vip))
  ```

- [ ] **Step 1.2:** Run the test against the **current (buggy)** implementation. Confirm the failures we expect.
  ```bash
  pytest tests/test_vip_formula.py -v
  ```
  Expected outcome:
  - `test_old_and_new_formulas_disagree_on_this_problem` — **PASS** (old vs new differ; this is a fixture sanity check, independent of `compute_vip`).
  - `test_compute_vip_matches_canonical_reference` — **FAIL** with an `AssertionError: Not equal to tolerance ...` message because `compute_vip` returns the buggy result.
  - `test_compute_vip_does_not_match_old_buggy_formula` — **FAIL** with `compute_vip() still matches the old (buggy) formula — fix not applied.`.
  - `test_canonical_invariant_average_squared_vip_is_one` — **FAIL** (the buggy formula does not satisfy this invariant).
  - `test_output_shape_and_nonnegativity` — **PASS** (shape and non-negativity are unaffected by the bug).
  
  Net: **3 failed, 2 passed**. If you see anything else (e.g. import error), fix that first before continuing.

- [ ] **Step 1.3:** Commit the failing test.
  ```bash
  git add tests/test_vip_formula.py
  git commit -m "test: add failing regression test for canonical VIP formula (T-05)"
  ```

## Task 2: Implement the canonical VIP formula

**Goal:** Replace the body of `compute_vip()` with the canonical Wold (2001) formula. Keep the function signature, name, and contract identical. Update the docstring with the citation.

**Files:**

- Modify: `src/spectral_predict/models.py` (function `compute_vip`, currently lines 1709-1751)

- [ ] **Step 2.1:** Replace the entire `compute_vip` function (from `def compute_vip(...)` through the final `return vip_scores`) with the version below. Use `Edit` to swap the exact block.

  **`old_string` (current buggy implementation):**
  ```python
  def compute_vip(pls_model, X, y):
      """
      Compute Variable Importance in Projection (VIP) scores for a fitted PLS model.

      Parameters
      ----------
      pls_model : PLSRegression or PLSTransformer
          Fitted PLS model
      X : array-like
          Training data
      y : array-like
          Target values

      Returns
      -------
      vip_scores : ndarray
          VIP score for each variable
      """
      # Handle PLSTransformer wrapper
      if isinstance(pls_model, PLSTransformer):
          pls_model = pls_model.pls_

      # Get PLS components
      W = pls_model.x_weights_  # (n_features, n_components)
      T = pls_model.x_scores_  # (n_samples, n_components)

      # Get explained variance by each component
      # SSY: sum of squares of y explained by each component
      y = np.asarray(y).reshape(-1, 1)
      ssy_comp = np.sum(T**2, axis=0) * np.var(y, axis=0)

      # Total SSY
      ssy_total = np.sum(ssy_comp)

      # VIP calculation (vectorized for performance)
      n_features = W.shape[0]
      n_components = W.shape[1]

      # Vectorized version: same math, but uses broadcasting instead of loop
      weight = np.sum((W ** 2) * ssy_comp, axis=1)  # Sum over components for each feature
      vip_scores = np.sqrt(n_features * weight / ssy_total)

      return vip_scores
  ```

  **`new_string` (canonical implementation):**
  ```python
  def compute_vip(pls_model, X, y):
      """
      Compute Variable Importance in Projection (VIP) scores for a fitted PLS model.

      Uses the canonical formula from Wold et al. (2001), as restated in
      Mehmood et al. (2012, Eq. 1):

          SSY_a   = q_a**2 * (T_a.T @ T_a)
          VIP_j   = sqrt( p * sum_a [ SSY_a * (W_{j,a} / ||W_a||)**2 ] / SSY_total )

      where ``W = x_weights_``, ``T = x_scores_``, ``q = y_loadings_[:, 0]``
      (univariate Y), and ``p = n_features``. The invariant
      ``mean(VIP**2) == 1`` follows from this definition.

      Parameters
      ----------
      pls_model : PLSRegression or PLSTransformer
          Fitted PLS model exposing ``x_weights_``, ``x_scores_``,
          ``y_loadings_``.
      X : array-like
          Training data. Currently unused (kept for API compatibility); VIP
          is fully determined by the fitted model's weights/scores/loadings.
      y : array-like
          Target values. Currently unused (kept for API compatibility); the
          per-component Y variance enters via ``y_loadings_``, not ``y``.

      Returns
      -------
      vip_scores : ndarray of shape (n_features,)
          VIP score for each variable.

      References
      ----------
      Wold, S., Sjöström, M., Eriksson, L. (2001). PLS-regression: a basic
      tool of chemometrics. *Chemometrics and Intelligent Laboratory
      Systems*, 58(2), 109-130.

      Mehmood, T., Liland, K. H., Snipen, L., Sæbø, S. (2012). A review of
      variable selection methods in Partial Least Squares Regression.
      *Chemometrics and Intelligent Laboratory Systems*, 118, 62-69.
      """
      # Handle PLSTransformer wrapper
      if isinstance(pls_model, PLSTransformer):
          pls_model = pls_model.pls_

      # Get PLS components
      W = np.asarray(pls_model.x_weights_)   # (n_features, n_components)
      T = np.asarray(pls_model.x_scores_)    # (n_samples, n_components)
      Q = np.asarray(pls_model.y_loadings_)  # (n_targets, n_components) for sklearn

      # Univariate Y: collapse Q to shape (n_components,)
      if Q.ndim == 1:
          q = Q
      else:
          q = Q[0, :]

      n_features = W.shape[0]

      # Per-component Y sum-of-squares: SSY_a = q_a**2 * sum_n T[n,a]**2
      ssy_comp = (q ** 2) * np.sum(T ** 2, axis=0)
      ssy_total = float(np.sum(ssy_comp))

      # Guard against degenerate fits (zero Y variance captured)
      if ssy_total <= 0.0:
          return np.zeros(n_features, dtype=float)

      # Normalized squared X-weights: (W_{j,a} / ||W_a||)**2
      # sklearn's PLSRegression already returns unit-norm columns of W, but
      # normalize defensively so the function is correct for any caller.
      col_norm_sq = np.sum(W ** 2, axis=0)
      col_norm_sq = np.where(col_norm_sq > 0.0, col_norm_sq, 1.0)
      w_norm_sq = (W ** 2) / col_norm_sq  # broadcasts (p, A) / (A,) -> (p, A)

      # VIP_j = sqrt( p * sum_a [ SSY_a * w_norm_sq[j, a] ] / SSY_total )
      vip_scores = np.sqrt(n_features * (w_norm_sq @ ssy_comp) / ssy_total)

      return vip_scores
  ```

- [ ] **Step 2.5:** Commit the implementation.
  ```bash
  git add src/spectral_predict/models.py
  git commit -m "fix(T-05): use canonical Wold (2001) VIP formula in compute_vip

  Pre-fix, compute_vip() weighted every PLS component by a single
  scalar np.var(y), so per-component Y-explained variance collapsed
  to sum(T_a**2) * const. This skewed VIP rankings on any problem
  with > 1 component, propagating into the GUI's wavelength
  importance display and the 'importance' variable-selection method.

  Replace with SSY_a = q_a**2 * (T_a.T @ T_a) using y_loadings_, per
  Wold et al. (2001) and Mehmood et al. (2012, Eq. 1). Verified by
  tests/test_vip_formula.py: matches an independent canonical
  reference, fails the old-formula match, and satisfies the textbook
  mean(VIP**2) == 1 invariant. Existing PLS-DA importance tests
  still pass."
  ```

  ### T-05: VIP formula fix
  Replaced `np.var(y)` per-component weight with canonical

  `src/spectral_predict/models.py:compute_vip`. Old formula collapsed all

  components had similar X-score energy but different Y-loading. New tests

## Non-goals / explicitly out of scope

- Changing the `compute_vip` signature (still takes `pls_model, X, y`). `X` and `y` become unused — documented in the docstring; not removed to keep the call sites in `get_feature_importances` and any test code unchanged.
- Multivariate-Y VIP. The canonical formula generalizes (sum over Y columns of `q_{k,a}**2`); this fix preserves the existing univariate-Y assumption (`Q[0, :]`) because every PLS call site in `spectral_predict/` passes a single response. If multi-Y support is added later, revisit.
- Re-running the full nightly grid search. VIP feeds the `importance` variable-selection path; downstream rankings *will* shift on real data, but that is the intended fix, not a regression. No baseline metrics are pinned anywhere in the suite that we need to update.
- Re-deriving or sanity-checking the VIP formula for non-sklearn PLS implementations beyond the defensive `||W_a||` normalization already added.

---

## Open questions for reviewer

1. Are there any other call sites that consume `compute_vip` output and pin numerical magnitudes (e.g. golden-standard fixtures, frozen result CSVs)? `tests/gold_standards/` was not exhaustively scanned — if any of those embed VIP numbers from the old formula, they will need refreshed values.
2. Should `compute_vip` drop the unused `X`, `y` parameters (signature change) or keep them for backward compatibility? This plan keeps them for compatibility; a follow-up can deprecate.
3. The plan does not run on the full `tests/` suite, only the targeted subset. Worth a full `pytest tests/ -x` before merging? (Probably yes, but it's outside this 30-min ticket's effort budget.)

    8: VIP_TEMPLATE = '''
    9: def compute_vip(X, y, n_components=None):
   10:     """
   11:     Compute VIP (Variable Importance in Projection) scores for PLS.
   12: 
   13:     VIP measures each variable's contribution to the PLS model.
   14:     Variables with VIP > 1 are typically considered important.
   15: 
   16:     Parameters
   17:     ----------
   18:     X : np.ndarray
   19:         Spectral data, shape (n_samples, n_wavelengths)
   20:     y : np.ndarray
   21:         Target values
   22:     n_components : int, optional
   23:         Number of PLS components. If None, auto-selects.
   24: 
   25:     Returns
   26:     -------
   27:     np.ndarray
   28:         VIP scores for each wavelength (higher = more important)
   29: 
   30:     Reference
   31:     ---------
   32:     Wold, S., Sjostrom, M., & Eriksson, L. (2001). PLS-regression: a basic
   33:     tool of chemometrics. Chemometrics and Intelligent Laboratory Systems,
   34:     58(2), 109-130.
   35:     """
   36:     from sklearn.cross_decomposition import PLSRegression
   37: 
   38:     X = np.asarray(X)
   39:     y = np.asarray(y).ravel()
   40:     n_samples, n_features = X.shape
   41: 
   42:     if n_components is None:
   43:         n_components = min(10, n_features // 2, n_samples // 2)
   44:     n_components = max(1, n_components)
   45: 
   46:     pls = PLSRegression(n_components=n_components)
   47:     pls.fit(X, y)
   48: 
   49:     W = pls.x_weights_  # (n_features, n_components)
   50:     T = pls.x_scores_   # (n_samples, n_components)
   51: 
   52:     # Explained variance per component
   53:     y_reshaped = y.reshape(-1, 1)
   54:     ssy_comp = np.sum(T**2, axis=0) * np.var(y_reshaped, axis=0)
   55:     ssy_total = np.sum(ssy_comp)
   56: 
   57:     if ssy_total < 1e-10:
   58:         return np.ones(n_features)
   59: 
   60:     # VIP calculation
   61:     weight = np.sum((W ** 2) * ssy_comp, axis=1)
   62:     vip_scores = np.sqrt(n_features * weight / ssy_total)
   63: 
   64:     return vip_scores
   65: 
   66: 
   67: def select_by_vip(X, y, n_variables, n_components=None):
   68:     """
   69:     Select top variables by VIP score.
   70: 
   71:     Parameters
   72:     ----------
   73:     X : np.ndarray
   74:         Spectral data
   75:     y : np.ndarray
   76:         Target values
   77:     n_variables : int
   78:         Number of variables to select
   79:     n_components : int, optional
   80:         Number of PLS components
   81: 
   82:     Returns
   83:     -------
   84:     np.ndarray
   85:         Indices of selected variables (sorted by importance, descending)
   86:     """
   87:     vip_scores = compute_vip(X, y, n_components)
   88:     selected_indices = np.argsort(vip_scores)[::-1][:n_variables]
   89:     return np.sort(selected_indices)
   90: '''

docs\T01_VARSEL_LEAKAGE_AUDIT.md:37:2. **Unified Bayesian path** — The `compute_importances()` function at `unified_bayesian.py:593` must be replaced with per-fold varsel inside the Optuna objective. This is architecturally harder than the grid fix because the objective already runs per-trial (not per-fold), and the importances are cached per preprocessing+method across trials. The fix: instead of caching importances computed on full data, cache per-fold varsel results keyed by `(preprocess, method, fold_train_indices_hash)`.

 succeeded in 383ms:
  971:     def _compute_vip(
  972:         self,
  973:         pls: PLSRegression,
  974:         X: np.ndarray,
  975:         y: np.ndarray
  976:     ) -> np.ndarray:
  977:         """Compute VIP (Variable Importance in Projection) scores."""
  978:         n_features = X.shape[1]
  979:         n_components = pls.n_components
  980: 
  981:         # Get weights and scores
  982:         W = pls.x_weights_
  983:         T = pls.x_scores_
  984:         Q = pls.y_loadings_
  985: 
  986:         # Handle Q shape: sklearn may return (n_components, n_targets) or transpose
  987:         # For 1D y, Q should be (n_components, 1) but let's handle both cases
  988:         if Q.shape[0] == 1 and Q.shape[1] == n_components:
  989:             # Q is transposed: (1, n_components) -> use Q[0, i]
  990:             Q = Q.T  # Now (n_components, 1)
  991: 
  992:         # Compute SS for each component
  993:         ss = np.zeros(n_components)
  994:         for i in range(n_components):
  995:             # Q[i, 0] is the y-loading for component i
  996:             q_val = Q[i, 0] if Q.shape[1] > 0 else Q[i] if Q.ndim == 1 else 1.0
  997:             ss[i] = (T[:, i].T @ T[:, i]) * (q_val**2)
  998: 
  999:         total_ss = np.sum(ss)
 1000: 
 1001:         # Compute VIP for each variable
 1002:         vip = np.zeros(n_features)
 1003:         for j in range(n_features):
 1004:             s = 0
 1005:             for i in range(n_components):
 1006:                 s += (W[j, i]**2) * ss[i]
 1007:             vip[j] = np.sqrt(n_features * s / (total_ss + 1e-10))
 1008: 
 1009:         return vip
 1010:

  145:     elif method == 'lightgbm':
  146:         importance = _compute_lightgbm_importance(X, y_encoded, task_type)
  147: 
  148:     elif method == 'vip':
  149:         importance = _compute_vip_importance(X, y_encoded)
  150: 
  151:     else:
  152:         raise ValueError(f"Unknown importance method: {method}")
  153: 
  154:     # Normalize to [0, 1]
  155:     importance = np.asarray(importance, dtype=float)
  156:     max_imp = importance.max()
  157:     if max_imp > 0:
  158:         importance = importance / max_imp
  159: 
  160:     return importance
  161: 
  162: 
  163: def _compute_cars_tree_importance(
  164:     X: np.ndarray,
  165:     y: np.ndarray,

402:       # Guard against degenerate fits (zero Y variance captured)
469: ## Task 3: Broader regression check

 succeeded in 366ms:
20: 
21: ```python
22: ssy_comp = np.sum(T**2, axis=0) * np.var(y, axis=0)   # WRONG
23: ```
24: 
25: This multiplies the sum of squared X-scores for each component by a single scalar (`np.var(y)`), so every component gets weighted by the **same** value. The component-axis spread is therefore driven entirely by `sum(T_a**2)` (the X-score energy), independent of how much **Y** each component actually explains. VIP collapses to a re-weighting of `W**2` by X-score energy, which is *not* VIP.
26: 
27: ### The canonical formula
28: 
29: The correct definition (Wold, 2001; Mehmood et al., 2012) is:
30: 
31: > Let `T` be the X-scores `(n_samples, A)`, `q` be the y-loadings `(A,)` (or `y_loadings_[:, 0]` for univariate Y in scikit-learn's `PLSRegression`), and `W` be the X-weights `(p, A)`.
32: >
33: > The Y variance explained by component `a` is:
34: >
35: >     SSY_a = q_a**2 * (T_a.T @ T_a)
36: >
37: > and the total explained Y variance is `SSY_total = sum_a SSY_a`.
38: >
39: > Then for variable `j`:
40: >
41: >     VIP_j = sqrt( p * sum_a [ SSY_a * (W_{j,a} / ||W_a||)**2 ] / SSY_total )
42: 
43: Notes:
44: 
45: - The `(W_{j,a} / ||W_a||)**2` term is the *normalized* squared X-weight for variable `j` on component `a`. With `sklearn.cross_decomposition.PLSRegression`, `x_weights_` columns are already unit-norm (`||W_a||==1`), so the normalization is a no-op for the sklearn case but we keep the explicit `/ ||W_a||**2` for correctness against any other PLS implementation that may pass through this function.
46: - `sum_j VIP_j**2 / p == 1` is the canonical sanity invariant ("average squared VIP equals 1") — used as a test.
47: 
48: ### Why fix it now
49: 
50: VIP is exposed in the GUI's wavelength-importance ranking (`get_feature_importances` -> `compute_vip` for `model_name in ("PLS","PLS-DA")`) and feeds the `importance` variable-selection method. A skewed VIP shifts which wavelengths get selected, which propagates through the entire model search. Roadmap effort estimate: **30 min**.
51: 
52: ### References
53: 
54: - Wold, S., Sjöström, M., Eriksson, L. (2001). "PLS-regression: a basic tool of chemometrics." *Chemometrics and Intelligent Laboratory Systems*, **58**(2), 109–130. https://doi.org/10.1016/S0169-7439(01)00155-1
55: - Mehmood, T., Liland, K. H., Snipen, L., Sæbø, S. (2012). "A review of variable selection methods in Partial Least Squares Regression." *Chemometrics and Intelligent Laboratory Systems*, **118**, 62–69. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.chemolab.2012.07.010 — see Eq. (1) for the VIP formula stated above.

The proposed code uses `Q[0, :]`, but the plan text/docstring would mislead an unattended implementer. Fix the text to `y_loadings_[0, :]`; for future multi-output, use `np.sum(Q**2, axis=0)`.
